12           Abstract [SOURCE: Page_2]

13   While the number of studies dealing with fine root dynamics in deep soils layers (depth > 1 [SOURCE: Page_2]

14   m) has increased sharply recently, the phenology, the morphology, the anatomy and the role [SOURCE: Page_2]

15   of deep fine roots are still poorly known in forest ecosystems. This review summarizes the [SOURCE: Page_2]

16   current knowledge on fine root production, mortality and longevity in deep soil layers, [SOURCE: Page_2]

17   mycorrhizal association with deep roots, and the role of deep fine roots on carbon, water and [SOURCE: Page_2]

18   nutrient cycling in forest ecosystems. Plant species are known to be more deeply rooted in [SOURCE: Page_2]

19   tropical ecosystems than in temperate and boreal ecosystems, but deep-rooted species are [SOURCE: Page_2]

20   common in a wide range of climates. Deep fine roots are highly plastic in response to changes [SOURCE: Page_2]

21   in environmental conditions and soil resources. Recent studies show that functional traits can [SOURCE: Page_2]

22   be different for deep and shallow roots, with a possible functional specialization of deep fine [SOURCE: Page_2]

23   roots to take up nutrients. With higher vessel diameter and larger tracheid, the anatomy of [SOURCE: Page_2]

24   deep fine roots is also oriented toward water acquisition and transport by increasing the [SOURCE: Page_2]

25   hydraulic conductivity. Deep fine roots can have a great impact on the biogeochemical cycles [SOURCE: Page_2]

26   in many forests (in particular in tropical areas where highly weathered soils are commonly [SOURCE: Page_2]

27   very deep), making it possible to take up water and nutrients over dry periods and [SOURCE: Page_2]

28   contributing to store carbon in the soil. The biogeochemical models in forest ecosystems need [SOURCE: Page_2]

29   to consider the specificity of deep root functioning to better predict carbon, water and nutrient [SOURCE: Page_2]

30   cycling as well as net ecosystem productivity. [SOURCE: Page_2]

31 [SOURCE: Page_2]

32   Key words: deep rooting, root growth, root traits, carbon sequestration, water uptake, nutrient [SOURCE: Page_2]

33   uptake, deep mycorrhizas [SOURCE: Page_2]

2 [SOURCE: Page_2]

34   Introduction [SOURCE: Page_3]

35   Roots have an essential role in forest ecosystems through the mechanical function of tree [SOURCE: Page_3]

36   anchorage and the physiological function of absorbing and transporting the amounts of water [SOURCE: Page_3]

37   and nutrients needed for plant development (Pregitzer et al., 2002; McCormack and Guo, [SOURCE: Page_3]

38   2014). The anchorage function is mainly enabled by physical interactions between the roots [SOURCE: Page_3]

39   and the soil. The absorptive function is more complex as it involves biogeochemical [SOURCE: Page_3]

40   interactions between fine roots, soil particles and soil biota, with sometimes symbioses [SOURCE: Page_3]

41   between fine roots and soil bacteria or fungi. Fine roots, commonly considered as root with a [SOURCE: Page_3]

42   diameter lower than 2 mm (McCormack et al., 2015), have the ability to adapt to changing [SOURCE: Page_3]

43   environments to facilitate plant development. Fine root activity can modify physical, chemical [SOURCE: Page_3]

44   and biological soil properties, which can have repercussions at the plant level but also at the [SOURCE: Page_3]

45   ecosystem level (McCormack et al., 2015). Indeed, fine roots play a major role in carbon (C) [SOURCE: Page_3]

46   cycling in forest ecosystems, with large C inputs into the soil through root exudates and [SOURCE: Page_3]

47   necromass production, as well as large C returns to the atmosphere through root respiration [SOURCE: Page_3]

48   and decomposition processes (Balesdent and Balabane, 1996; Strand et al., 2008). The great [SOURCE: Page_3]

49   plasticity of root systems, both architecturally and functionally, can be strongly influenced by [SOURCE: Page_3]

50   plant phenology, soil properties and climatic conditions (Hodge et al., 2009). Fine root [SOURCE: Page_3]

51   dynamics have been studied quantifying production and mortality (Hendrick and Pregitzer, [SOURCE: Page_3]

52   1993; McCormack and Guo, 2014), turnover and survivorship (Anderson et al., 2003), [SOURCE: Page_3]

53   senescence (Huck et al., 1987) and elongation rates (Germon et al., 2016; Lambais et al., [SOURCE: Page_3]

54   2017). Fine root dynamics are driven by exogenous factors like soil temperature and water [SOURCE: Page_3]

55   content (Coll et al., 2012) and endogenous factors like hormone productions (McAdam et al., [SOURCE: Page_3]

56   2016) and photosynthate and nutrient allocations within the plant (Tierney and Fahey, 2002; [SOURCE: Page_3]

57   Sloan et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_3]

3 [SOURCE: Page_3]

58   Binkley (2015) pointed out the critical need to include depth and timescale of ecological [SOURCE: Page_4]

59   processes below the soil surface in a modern conceptualization of forest ecosystems. The full [SOURCE: Page_4]

60   ‘critical zone’ concept from Earth science should be included in conventional thinking about [SOURCE: Page_4]

61   ecosystems and deep roots can be a key functional element of the Critical Zone (Richter and [SOURCE: Page_4]

62   Billings, 2015; Pierret et al., 2016). Shrub and tree roots have the ability to explore huge [SOURCE: Page_4]

63   volumes of soil and can be found at great depths in xeric environments (Canadell et al., 1996; [SOURCE: Page_4]

64   Schenk and Jackson, 2002). Deep roots improve tree tolerance to drought (McDowell et al., [SOURCE: Page_4]

65   2008; Nardini et al., 2016) and store C in deep soil layers (Balesdent et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_4]

66   Understanding the spatial and temporal variability of fine root functioning in deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_4]

67   is needed to improve our understanding of biosphere-atmosphere interactions (Zeng et al., [SOURCE: Page_4]

68   1998; Kleidon and Heimann, 2000), and the accuracy of terrestrial biosphere models (Saleska [SOURCE: Page_4]

69   et al., 2007; Christoffersen et al., 2014). Even though the importance of deep root systems has [SOURCE: Page_4]

70   been highlighted in several reviews focused on trees (Canadell et al., 1996; Maeght et al., [SOURCE: Page_4]

71   2013; Pierret et al., 2016) or on crops (Thorup-Kristensen et al., 2020), the functional [SOURCE: Page_4]

72   significance of deep fine root dynamics, and their contribution to biogeochemical cycles are [SOURCE: Page_4]

73   still poorly documented. Maeght et al. (2013) proposed to consider as deep roots the roots [SOURCE: Page_4]

74   growing at a depth of more than 1 m. Even though fine root densities are low at great depths, [SOURCE: Page_4]

75   deep roots are likely to play a key role in many processes of plant physiology, community [SOURCE: Page_4]

76   ecology and biogeochemistry in forest ecosystems. Deep roots are found in many biomes [SOURCE: Page_4]

77   where drought periods can occur (Nepstad et al., 1994; Canadell et al., 1996; Jackson et al., [SOURCE: Page_4]

78   2000; Schenk and Jackson, 2002). However, the maximum rooting depth reported in the [SOURCE: Page_4]

79   scientific literature is commonly underestimated because most studies did not sample soils [SOURCE: Page_4]

80   down to the root front, which artificially truncated root distributions (Freycon et al., 2015). In [SOURCE: Page_4]

81   a review paper, Stone and Kalisz (1991) reported 37 examples of root systems observed [SOURCE: Page_4]

82   between 10 and 60 m below the soil surface, indicating that the maximum rooting depth can [SOURCE: Page_4]

4 [SOURCE: Page_4]

83   vary considerably with climatic and soil conditions as well as between tree species. In another [SOURCE: Page_5]

84    review paper, Canadell et al. (1996) estimated that roots extend in deep horizons down to 9.5 [SOURCE: Page_5]

85    ± 2.4 m in deserts, 7.3 ± 2.8 m in tropical evergreen forest and 15.0 ± 5.4 m in tropical [SOURCE: Page_5]

86    grassland and savannah, with a maximum rooting depth of 68 m reported for Boscia [SOURCE: Page_5]

87    albitrunca trees in Botswana. Schenk and Jackson (2002) showed in a meta-analysis that the [SOURCE: Page_5]

88    maximum rooting depth increased for plants growth forms as their size and life span [SOURCE: Page_5]

89    increased, with the highest values for trees and shrubs (Figure 1). [SOURCE: Page_5]

90    Distributions and dynamics of deep roots are still poorly documented relative to shallow roots [SOURCE: Page_5]

91    (Richter and Billings, 2015; Pierret et al., 2016). Despite recent technical innovations, the [SOURCE: Page_5]

92   observation of deep roots remains difficult, time-consuming, expensive and requires a [SOURCE: Page_5]

93   combination of methods to obtain reliable results (Maeght et al., 2013). Direct and indirect [SOURCE: Page_5]

94   techniques can be used for monitoring deep roots depending on the research aim (Vogt et al., [SOURCE: Page_5]

95   1998; Hendricks et al., 2006). Deep root functioning at considerable soil depths has been [SOURCE: Page_5]

96   indirectly studied by quantifying water, nitrogen and carbon budgets of the ecosystem (Vogt [SOURCE: Page_5]

97   et al., 1998). Direct methods based on soil excavation, trenches, sequential coring, ingrowth [SOURCE: Page_5]

98   cores, and rhizotron observations have been used to monitor root dynamics in situ (Maeght et [SOURCE: Page_5]

99   al., 2013). Both indirect and direct approaches can have potential biases leading to [SOURCE: Page_5]

100   overestimate or underestimate root growth and root decomposition. There is no consensus on [SOURCE: Page_5]

101   the most effective and reliable method that should be used to quantify fine root dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_5]

102   Several attempts in plant root architecture and development modelling were performed so far [SOURCE: Page_5]

103   without considering deep rooting patterns. Some models were based on a continuous [SOURCE: Page_5]

104   formalism through partial derivative equations that simulate root apex density and root [SOURCE: Page_5]

105   interaction through time and space (Bastian et al., 2008; Dupuy et al., 2010; Bonneu et al., [SOURCE: Page_5]

106   2012). These types of models enabled direct coupling with water transport models (Doussan [SOURCE: Page_5]

107   et al., 1998), or nutrient uptake competition models (Bonneu et al., 2012; Mayer et al., 2012; [SOURCE: Page_5]

5 [SOURCE: Page_5]

108   Gérard et al., 2017). Other models were based on a discrete and/or random formalism that [SOURCE: Page_6]

109   simulates root architecture, growth, branching and death processes. Some of them were based [SOURCE: Page_6]

110   on mathematical processes such as L-systems (Leitner et al., 2010) or fractals (van Noordwijk [SOURCE: Page_6]

111   et al., 1994; Ozier-Lafontaine et al., 1999), or using combinations of the two (Shibusawa, [SOURCE: Page_6]

112   1994), others were derived from direct measurements performed on real plants taking into [SOURCE: Page_6]

113   account root-soil interaction functions (Diggle, 1988; Jourdan and Rey, 1997a, b; Lynch et [SOURCE: Page_6]

114   al., 1997; Pagès et al., 2004; Dunbabin et al., 2013; Pagès et al., 2014; Barczi et al., 2018), [SOURCE: Page_6]

115   with water and nutrient uptake (Wu et al., 2007; Javaux et al., 2008; Leitner et al., 2010; [SOURCE: Page_6]

116   Postma et al., 2017). Most of these models, with the exception of some of them (Jourdan and [SOURCE: Page_6]

117   Rey, 1997a, b; Pagès et al., 2014; Barczi et al., 2018), are mainly designed for annual and [SOURCE: Page_6]

118   herbaceous plants, with a short lifespan and a limited rooting depth. For perennial crops, [SOURCE: Page_6]

119   shrubs and trees with longer lifespans and deeper rooting strategies, previous models do not [SOURCE: Page_6]

120   consider possible changes in root architecture, diameter, growth, mortality, or functioning [SOURCE: Page_6]

121   processes depending on (great) soil depth. A first attempt was made with the DigR model, [SOURCE: Page_6]

122   which manages parameter values that may change depending on the position along roots of [SOURCE: Page_6]

123   each type, changing with soil depth or not (Barczi et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_6]

124   Our paper provides an up-to-date literature review on morphological and phenological traits [SOURCE: Page_6]

125   of fine roots along deep soil profiles in forest ecosystems. The production, mortality rates and [SOURCE: Page_6]

126   longevity of deep fine roots, as well as symbiosis with mycorrhizas, their role in water and [SOURCE: Page_6]

127   nutrient uptake and their influence on belowground C sequestration are reviewed. We [SOURCE: Page_6]

128   addressed the following questions: 1) How can environmental conditions, soil resources and [SOURCE: Page_6]

129   symbiotic association along deep soil profiles influence the functioning of fine roots and their [SOURCE: Page_6]

130   main functional traits? 2) Do we need to take into consideration the specificity of deep fine [SOURCE: Page_6]

131   roots to improve current predictions of C, water and nutrient cycling in forest ecosystems? We [SOURCE: Page_6]

6 [SOURCE: Page_6]

132   proposed a conceptual scheme summarizing how root traits vary between the topsoil and deep [SOURCE: Page_7]

133   soil layers (Figure 2). [SOURCE: Page_7]

7 [SOURCE: Page_7]

134               1. Morphology and anatomy of deep fine roots [SOURCE: Page_8]

135   The morphology and architecture of root systems vary greatly between plant species but also [SOURCE: Page_8]

136   within individual species (Cannon, 1949; Nibau et al., 2008). Biogeochemical processes in [SOURCE: Page_8]

137   forest ecosystems are strongly influenced by root architecture (Addo-Danso et al., 2020), as [SOURCE: Page_8]

138   well as the ability of plants to survive under stressful conditions (Penuelas et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_8]

139   Functional traits of fine roots vary with environmental conditions and resource distribution [SOURCE: Page_8]

140   (Ostonen et al., 2007). Fine roots have been classified depending on their main functions and [SOURCE: Page_8]

141   metabolic activity either in absorptive roots involved in the capture and uptake of soil [SOURCE: Page_8]

142   resources that are extremely fine and are the most distal roots, or in roots involved in the [SOURCE: Page_8]

143   transport of water and nutrients that are larger roots, higher in the branching order, and [SOURCE: Page_8]

144   constitute most of the structural part of the root system (McCormack et al., 2015). The high [SOURCE: Page_8]

145   variability of environmental conditions such as soil moisture and temperature or resource [SOURCE: Page_8]

146   (water and nutrients) availability along soil profiles result in contrasting fine root functional [SOURCE: Page_8]

147   traits with soil depth. [SOURCE: Page_8]

148   Fine roots have been commonly considered as a coherent “pool” of an arbitrary 0-2 mm root [SOURCE: Page_8]

149   diameter class with the same dynamics, turnover rates, respiration costs and the same rates of [SOURCE: Page_8]

150   water and nutrient uptake (Persson, 1980; Jackson et al., 1997). However, an increasing [SOURCE: Page_8]

151   number of recent studies suggest that fine root functions can vary depending on the root order [SOURCE: Page_8]

152   of the branching root system (Pregitzer et al., 1997; Majdi et al., 2001; Pregitzer et al., 2002; [SOURCE: Page_8]

153   Guo et al., 2004; Wang et al., 2006; Hishi, 2007; Wang et al., 2007; Pregitzer, 2008; [SOURCE: Page_8]

154   Valenzuela Estrada et al., 2008; Comas and Eissenstat, 2009; Fan and Guo, 2010; Rewald et [SOURCE: Page_8]

155   al., 2011; Beyer et al., 2013; Rewald et al., 2018; Wada et al., 2019). These studies have [SOURCE: Page_8]

156   clearly shown that several fine root traits such as diameter (D), length (L), specific root length [SOURCE: Page_8]

157   (SRL), specific root area (SRA), root length density (RLD), branching density but also [SOURCE: Page_8]

158   number of xylem vessels, tissue density, C/N ratios, phenolic, lignin and suberin contents, [SOURCE: Page_8]

8 [SOURCE: Page_8]

159   mycorrhizal colonization and median lifespans varied depending on the root order. The [SOURCE: Page_9]

160   highest fine root order number, the coarsest root diameter, the highest median lifespan, tissue [SOURCE: Page_9]

161   density, xylem vessel number, lignin and suberin content, and the lowest length, branching [SOURCE: Page_9]

162   density, RLD, SRL, SRA, C/N ratios, and mycorrhizal colonization. [SOURCE: Page_9]

163   Fine root exploration in deep soil layers represents a high metabolic cost for plants (Iversen, [SOURCE: Page_9]

164   2010), which may influence fine root traits (Prieto et al., 2015). Specific root length (SRL) [SOURCE: Page_9]

165   and specific root area (SRA) can be affected by soil depth (Maurice et al., 2010; Prieto et al., [SOURCE: Page_9]

166   2015; Pinheiro et al., 2016), while other functional traits (concentrations of C, N, cellulose, [SOURCE: Page_9]

167   lignin,…) seem less sensitive (Prieto et al., 2015). SRL and SRA values are positively [SOURCE: Page_9]

168   correlated with the soil volume in contact with fine roots, which influences the plant capacity [SOURCE: Page_9]

169   to take up limited soil resources (Addo-Danso et al., 2020). Changes in specific root length [SOURCE: Page_9]

170   and specific root area with soil depth have been little investigated and the trends are not clear [SOURCE: Page_9]

171   (Makita et al., 2011; Prieto et al., 2015; Pinheiro et al., 2016). Pinheiro et al. (2016) showed a [SOURCE: Page_9]

172   slight increase in SRL and SRA with soil depth down to 13.5 m for 4 genotypes of several [SOURCE: Page_9]

173   Eucalyptus species in Brazil. Maurice et al. (2010) also found that SRL increased with soil [SOURCE: Page_9]

174   depth (down to 3 m) in Eucalyptus plantations of different ages. On the contrary, SRL [SOURCE: Page_9]

175   decreased with soil depth down to 1.2 m in Pinus pinaster plantations in France (Bakker et [SOURCE: Page_9]

176   al., 2009). Adriano et al. (2017) found no significant changes in SRL with soil depth down to [SOURCE: Page_9]

177   8 m in Citrus sinensis plantations, but they observed a tendency toward an increase in mean [SOURCE: Page_9]

178   fine root diameters. Gwenzi et al. (2011) also found that fine root thickness tended to increase [SOURCE: Page_9]

179   with soil depth (down to 1.4 m) in Acacia rostellifera and Melaleuca nesophila plantations in [SOURCE: Page_9]

180   Western Australia. Prieto et al. (2015) measured fine root functional traits in 20 plant [SOURCE: Page_9]

181   communities under tropical climate, montane climate, and sub-humid Mediterranean climate. [SOURCE: Page_9]

182   They showed that fine roots were thicker in deep soil layers (down to 1.6 m) compared to the [SOURCE: Page_9]

183   topsoil. On the contrary, Sochacki et al. (2017) observed that root diameter decreased with [SOURCE: Page_9]

9 [SOURCE: Page_9]

184   depth down to 6 m in Eucalyptus globulus plantations in southwest Australia. Mixing tree [SOURCE: Page_10]

185   species can also greatly influence SRL and SLA but studies comparing fine root traits in [SOURCE: Page_10]

186   mixture with the monospecific stands along deep soil profiles are scarce. Germon et al. (2018) [SOURCE: Page_10]

187   showed that SRL and SRA values in mixed-species stands of Acacia mangium and Eucalyptus [SOURCE: Page_10]

188   grandis were 110–150% and 34% higher, respectively, than in their monoculture, and the [SOURCE: Page_10]

189   same pattern was observed between the topsoil and a depth of 6 m. Fast-growing species [SOURCE: Page_10]

190   require a rapid and efficient acquisition of resources above and belowground, which generally [SOURCE: Page_10]

191   translate into higher SRA and SRL values compared to slow-growing species, in particular in [SOURCE: Page_10]

192   deep soil layers (Ryser, 2006; Reich, 2014; Jo et al., 2015). High SRL and thin roots are often [SOURCE: Page_10]

193   associated with a better capacity to capture soil resources (Hodge, 2004). Establishing a deep [SOURCE: Page_10]

194   root system with a high area of contact between soil and fine roots (through high fine root [SOURCE: Page_10]

195   density and SRA values) makes it possible to withdraw water from a large soil volume [SOURCE: Page_10]

196   (Jackson et al., 2000). [SOURCE: Page_10]

197   Deep roots are mainly oriented toward water acquisition and transport. McElrone et al. (2004) [SOURCE: Page_10]

198   showed that deep roots of Juniperus ashei, Bumelia lanuginosa, Quercus fusiformis and [SOURCE: Page_10]

199   Quercus sinuate had higher vessel diameter and larger tracheid than shallow roots in deep soil [SOURCE: Page_10]

200   layers, down to a depth of 18-20 m, with higher hydraulic conductivity (Figure 3). Similar [SOURCE: Page_10]

201   results were found for eucalypt fine roots in Brazil, where xylem vessels were less abundant [SOURCE: Page_10]

202   but with a larger diameter at depth > 10 m than shallow roots of the same diameter class [SOURCE: Page_10]

203   (Perron-Gomide et al., Com. Pers. 2020). In line with those results, Pate et al. (1995) showed [SOURCE: Page_10]

204   that roots at a depth of 2 m had larger xylem conduits, higher SRL and SRA values, as well as [SOURCE: Page_10]

205   hydraulic conductivities up to 15 times higher than roots in shallow layers for tree and shrub [SOURCE: Page_10]

206   species of Proteaceae (Banksia prionotes, Banksia ilicifolia and Adenanthos cygnorum) in [SOURCE: Page_10]

207   Australia. Physiological and anatomical adjustments of fine roots in deep soil layers might be [SOURCE: Page_10]

208   an adaptation of plants to maximize their hydraulic efficiency (McElrone et al., 2007). Even [SOURCE: Page_10]

10 [SOURCE: Page_10]

209   those promising results suggesting that very deep roots, despite their scarcity, could have a [SOURCE: Page_11]

210   key role to take up water during drought periods, studies dealing with fine root morphology [SOURCE: Page_11]

211   and anatomy at great depth are still scarce. [SOURCE: Page_11]

212               2. Phenology of deep fine roots [SOURCE: Page_11]

213   Fine roots in deep and shallow soil layers exhibit contrasting phenology with an asynchrony [SOURCE: Page_11]

214   of growth. Maeght et al. (2015) showed that shallow fine root growth of Hevea brasiliensis [SOURCE: Page_11]

215   was synchronized with rainfall events and ceased during the dry season. On the contrary, fine [SOURCE: Page_11]

216   root growth occurred at the end of the wet season and continued over the dry season at a depth [SOURCE: Page_11]

217   of more than 2.5 m. Germon et al. (2016) also observed an asynchrony between the growth of [SOURCE: Page_11]

218   shallow and deep roots in an agroforestry system with walnut and durum wheat in the south of [SOURCE: Page_11]

219   France. While the growth of tree roots in the topsoil occurred mainly in summer, root growth [SOURCE: Page_11]

220   during the rainy period (in winter) was mainly observed at a depth of more than 2.5 m. The [SOURCE: Page_11]

221   same behavior has been shown in tropical Eucalyptus plantations growing in very deep soils [SOURCE: Page_11]

222   in Brazil. An asynchrony between shallow and deep root growth was observed, with fine root [SOURCE: Page_11]

223   growth occurring in the topsoil over the rainy summer while it peaked deeper than 3 m at the [SOURCE: Page_11]

224   end of the dry season (Lambais et al., 2017). In another Eucalyptus grandis stand, Germon et [SOURCE: Page_11]

225   al. (2019) showed a negative correlation between the amount of extractable water in the [SOURCE: Page_11]

226   topsoil and the flushes of fine root growth in very deep soil layers. In those plantations, fine [SOURCE: Page_11]

227   root growth in deep soil layers seems to be related to the overall water demand of the whole [SOURCE: Page_11]

228   tree and controlled by the need to use increasingly deeper water resources when water [SOURCE: Page_11]

229   becomes scarce in the topsoil, which is consistent with modeling studies (Christina et al., [SOURCE: Page_11]

230   2017; Christina et al., 2018). Vertical growth rates of trees were almost symmetrical above- [SOURCE: Page_11]

231   and below-ground in Eucalyptus plantations, reaching 10.4 and 19.2 m in height and a [SOURCE: Page_11]

232   maximum rooting depth of 9.2 and 15.8 m at 1.5 and 3.5 years after planting, respectively [SOURCE: Page_11]

233   (Christina et al., 2011). Other studies using different methodologies (deep drilling, [SOURCE: Page_11]

11 [SOURCE: Page_11]

234   observations on trench walls, injection of 15N tracer) in the same area confirmed this velocity [SOURCE: Page_12]

235   of root front displacement (Laclau et al., 2013b; Pinheiro et al., 2016; Pinheiro et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_12]

236   The growth rates of Eucalyptus roots observed on minirhizotrons by Lambais et al. (2017) are [SOURCE: Page_12]

237   consistent with this downward speed of exploration, with mean elongation rates increasing [SOURCE: Page_12]

238   with soil depth up to a maximum value of 3.6 cm day-1 over a short period below 3 m. In [SOURCE: Page_12]

239   another Eucalyptus grandis stand, minirhizotron observations confirmed higher fine root [SOURCE: Page_12]

240   elongation rates in deep soil layers than in the topsoil (Figure 4), reaching a maximum of 4.3 [SOURCE: Page_12]

241   cm per day over a short period at depths below 5 m (Laclau et al., 2013b; Germon et al., [SOURCE: Page_12]

242   2016; Lambais et al., 2017; Germon et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_12]

243   Increasing root growth rates with soil depth could be an adaptation to drought (Annerose and [SOURCE: Page_12]

244   Cornaire, 1994), providing a rapid access to large amounts of water stored in deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_12]

245   (Christina et al., 2017). This pattern suggests a positive feedback from root growth downward [SOURCE: Page_12]

246   to shoot growth (Friend et al., 1994). Environmental conditions and plant growth regulators [SOURCE: Page_12]

247   may control root exploration of deep soil layers (López-Bucio et al., 2003). Soil water [SOURCE: Page_12]

248   contents are commonly higher in deep soil layers and variations of temperature are lower than [SOURCE: Page_12]

249   in the topsoil (Du and Fang, 2014; Radville et al., 2016). Climatic factors are buffered in deep [SOURCE: Page_12]

250   soil layers, fluctuating less throughout the day and the seasons than in the upper layers, which [SOURCE: Page_12]

251   influences differentially root phenology along deep soil profiles. Changes in fine root [SOURCE: Page_12]

252   phenology with soil depth might also be controlled by other factors. As deep roots are more [SOURCE: Page_12]

253   distant from the shoot than shallow roots, photosynthates might take more time to reach deep [SOURCE: Page_12]

254   roots. [SOURCE: Page_12]

255   The longevity of fine roots generally increases with soil depth (Hendrick and Pregitzer, 1996; [SOURCE: Page_12]

256   Baddeley and Watson, 2005) but root behavior at depth may be different in some situations. [SOURCE: Page_12]

257   Wells et al. (2002) showed that root longevity of Prunus persica significantly increased with [SOURCE: Page_12]

258   soil depth down to 1.6 m. Germon et al. (2016) showed that the mortality of walnut roots was [SOURCE: Page_12]

12 [SOURCE: Page_12]

259   higher in the topsoil that in deep soil layers with a median life span of 167 days within the [SOURCE: Page_13]

260   first soil meter and 208 days in the 2.5-4.7 m layer in an agroforestry system in the south of [SOURCE: Page_13]

261   France. On the contrary, Maeght et al. (2015) found that root lifespan decreased with soil [SOURCE: Page_13]

262   depth in a rubber tree plantation in Thailand, from 180-250 days between the depth of 1 and [SOURCE: Page_13]

263   2.5 m to 120 days between 3 and 4.5 m. Lambais et al. (2017) found no significant differences [SOURCE: Page_13]

264   in the lifespan of Eucalyptus fine roots between the topsoil and a depth of 6 m in Brazil. The [SOURCE: Page_13]

265   relationship between depth and fine root longevity is therefore probably dependent on the [SOURCE: Page_13]

266   ecophysiology of tree species as well as on local environmental parameters (e.g., temperature, [SOURCE: Page_13]

267   water content, N availability, CO$_2$ and probably O$_2$ partial pressure) (Vogt et al., 1995). There [SOURCE: Page_13]

268   are still major uncertainties about the mechanisms controlling fine root phenology in deep soil [SOURCE: Page_13]

269   layers (Radville et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_13]

270               3. Fine root biomass and carbon sequestration in deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_13]

271   Soil C storage may be strongly underestimated because most studies have focused on the [SOURCE: Page_13]

272   topsoil (Harrison et al., 2011; Callesen et al., 2016). While fine roots play a major role in the [SOURCE: Page_13]

273   global carbon cycle, their contribution on soil C storage in very deep soil layers remains little [SOURCE: Page_13]

274   understood (Fontaine et al., 2007; Balesdent et al., 2018). Fine root biomass deeper than 1 m [SOURCE: Page_13]

275   is generally a small proportion of the total biomass in forest ecosystems (Schenk and Jackson, [SOURCE: Page_13]

276   2002; Pierret et al., 2016). However, the huge C stocks in deep soil layers worldwide suggest [SOURCE: Page_13]

277   that fine root dynamics could have a strong impact, on the long-term (Rumpel and Kögel- [SOURCE: Page_13]

278   Knabner, 2011; Stahl et al., 2016). Fine roots account for large C inputs to the soil through the [SOURCE: Page_13]

279   release of exudates and root necromass and large amounts of C return to the atmosphere [SOURCE: Page_13]

280   through root respiration and decomposition processes (Balesdent and Balabane, 1996; Strand [SOURCE: Page_13]

281   et al., 2008). Estimating fine root production and turnover throughout the entire soil profile is [SOURCE: Page_13]

282   needed to predict more accurately the potential role of forest ecosystems to mitigate climate [SOURCE: Page_13]

283   change. [SOURCE: Page_13]

13 [SOURCE: Page_13]

284   Even though fine root densities sharply decrease with soil depth, the root biomass at a depth [SOURCE: Page_14]

285   of more than 1 m depth has probably been underestimated. Maeght et al. (2015) showed that [SOURCE: Page_14]

286   more than 50% of the total fine root biomass was below 1 m depth in a rubber plantation in [SOURCE: Page_14]

287   Thailand. Fine root biomass between the depths of 1.0 and 4.0 m was 5.8 t ha-1 while it [SOURCE: Page_14]

288   amounted to only 4.8 t ha-1 in the 0-1 m soil layer. Soil coring and measurements of fine root [SOURCE: Page_14]

289   intersects on deep soil trenches showed a similar pattern in Brazil for several Eucalyptus [SOURCE: Page_14]

290   species with about 50% of the total fine root length at a depth of more than 1 m, from 1 to 6 [SOURCE: Page_14]

291   years after planting (Laclau et al., 2013b; Pinheiro et al., 2016). In an agroforestry system in [SOURCE: Page_14]

292   the south of France, Cardinael et al. (2015) showed that 35% of the total fine root intersects of [SOURCE: Page_14]

293   walnut trees on trench walls were deeper than 2 m. At the same site, using the minirhizotron [SOURCE: Page_14]

294   technique, Germon et al. (2016) measured 25% of the total fine root production deeper than 4 [SOURCE: Page_14]

295   m. Germon et al. (2018) showed that the proportion of fine root biomass in the 0-1 m layer [SOURCE: Page_14]

296   relative to the total fine root biomass down to 17 m was 31% in Eucalyptus grandis stands, [SOURCE: Page_14]

297   52% in Acacia mangium stands and 37% in a mixture with 50% of the two species, at 4 years [SOURCE: Page_14]

298   after planting. The proportion of deep roots increases with stand age in young stands. Fine [SOURCE: Page_14]

299   roots below 1 m represented 10 %, 30% and 42% of the total fine root biomass in 2-, 4- and [SOURCE: Page_14]

300   12-year-old Ziziphus jujube plantations in China (Ma et al., 2013). Fine root intersects on [SOURCE: Page_14]

301   trench walls at a depth of more than 4 m represented 5%, 10% and 15% of total fine roots in [SOURCE: Page_14]

302   1-, 3.5- and 6-year-old Eucalyptus plantation in Brazil (Laclau et al., 2013b). The rooting [SOURCE: Page_14]

303   depth also increased with stand age down to a maximum of 23.2 m at age 22 years in apple [SOURCE: Page_14]

304   orchards in a sub-humid region of China, and roots deeper than 1 m accounted for 49% of the [SOURCE: Page_14]

305   total fine root biomass at 22 years (Li et al., 2019). However, the increase of root biomass in [SOURCE: Page_14]

306   deep soil layers can decrease water availability in sub-humid or arid climates (Figure 5). [SOURCE: Page_14]

307   A recent meta-analysis of changes in stable carbon isotope signatures across different climatic [SOURCE: Page_14]

308   zones (at 112 sites) showed that the proportion of carbon allocated deeper than 30 cm was [SOURCE: Page_14]

14 [SOURCE: Page_14]

309   much higher than the proportion of root biomass documented in meta-analyses (Balesdent et [SOURCE: Page_15]

310   al., 2018). This pattern could be explained by the contribution of root exudates in addition to [SOURCE: Page_15]

311   root mortality as well as by reduced decay rates at depth. Several interacting processes could [SOURCE: Page_15]

312   contribute to slow down decay rates at depth: scattered microbial biomass, stabilization of soil [SOURCE: Page_15]

313   organic matter by minerals, as well as a reduced stimulation of SOC decomposition by the [SOURCE: Page_15]

314   supply of fresh carbon (priming effect) (Fontaine et al., 2007). Fine root decomposition rates [SOURCE: Page_15]

315   influence the ability of forest ecosystems to store C and act as a sink for CO$_2$. When roots are [SOURCE: Page_15]

316   decomposed, parts of root tissues are incorporated into the soil organic matter (Strand et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

317   2008). De Camargo et al. (1999) showed that fine roots can be decomposed at least down to a [SOURCE: Page_15]

318   depth of 5 m in eastern Amazon. Nepstad et al. (1994) estimated that C stocks deeper than 1 [SOURCE: Page_15]

319   m exceeded SOC in the 0-1 m layer and the above-ground organic C in an Amazonian [SOURCE: Page_15]

320   tropical forest. The deep root system of this Amazonian forest probably released large [SOURCE: Page_15]

321   amounts of organic C into the soil through root exudation, mycorrhizal associations and fine [SOURCE: Page_15]

322   root mortality (Nepstad et al., 1994). Moreover, the microbial biomass is much lower in deep [SOURCE: Page_15]

323   soil layers than in the topsoil, which in combination with oxygen limitations could enhance [SOURCE: Page_15]

324   organic C sequestration as a result of low mineralization rates (Taylor et al., 2002; Rumpel [SOURCE: Page_15]

325   and Kögel-Knabner, 2011). Organic C derived from roots is generally more sequestered in the [SOURCE: Page_15]

326   soil than organic C coming from the aboveground litter, which is more affected by [SOURCE: Page_15]

327   physicochemical processes and has a different structural composition (Rasse et al., 2005; [SOURCE: Page_15]

328   Schmidt et al., 2011; Menichetti et al., 2015). Further studies investigating the relationships [SOURCE: Page_15]

329   between fine root dynamics, microbial communities and soil environment throughout very [SOURCE: Page_15]

330   deep soil profiles in contrasting pedo-climatic conditions are needed to improve the [SOURCE: Page_15]

331   predictions of soil C storage in deep soil layers under climate change. [SOURCE: Page_15]

332               4. Mycorrhizal association with deep roots [SOURCE: Page_15]

15 [SOURCE: Page_15]

333   Symbiotic association with root systems involving mycorrhizal fungi are common in forest [SOURCE: Page_16]

334   ecosystems (Högberg and Högberg, 2002; Churchland and Grayston, 2014), with beneficial [SOURCE: Page_16]

335   effects both for plants and fungi. Plants provide carbon to mycorrhizal fungi and in exchange, [SOURCE: Page_16]

336   mycorrhizas greatly increase the volume of soil explored, which makes it possible to increase [SOURCE: Page_16]

337   the amounts of water and nutrients taken up in the soil and transferred to the plants (Johnson [SOURCE: Page_16]

338   and Gehring, 2007). Symbiotic associations between deep roots and mycorrhizal fungi are [SOURCE: Page_16]

339   poorly documented and until recently, it was commonly believed that mycorrhizal [SOURCE: Page_16]

340   associations were limited to shallow soil layers. Nevertheless, recent researches on microbial [SOURCE: Page_16]

341   communities have highlighted the value of studying deep soil layers and showed unexpected [SOURCE: Page_16]

342   abundant and diverse microbial communities in subsoils (Li et al., 2014; Gocke et al., 2017; [SOURCE: Page_16]

343   Zheng et al., 2017; Pereira et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_16]

344   Most of research about arbuscular mycorrhizal (AM) fungi depending on soil depth has been [SOURCE: Page_16]

345   carried out in agricultural systems down to a maximum depth of 1 meter. The results were [SOURCE: Page_16]

346   mainly focused on the abundance and diversity of AM spores, with few studies taking into [SOURCE: Page_16]

347   account AM fungi root colonization (Kabir et al., 1998; Oehl et al., 2005; Cuenca and Lovera, [SOURCE: Page_16]

348   2010; Yang et al., 2010; Taniguchi et al., 2012; Shukla et al., 2013; Becerra et al., 2014; Gai [SOURCE: Page_16]

349   et al., 2015). Nevertheless, some older studies report observations of mycorrhizal associations [SOURCE: Page_16]

350   more deeply, down to a depth of 4 m (Virginia et al., 1986; Zajicek et al., 1986). In tropical [SOURCE: Page_16]

351   agroforests with Faidherbia albida trees, Dalpé et al. (2000) showed that AM propagules [SOURCE: Page_16]

352   were viable in cores taken down to a depth of 34 m, which highlights the importance of [SOURCE: Page_16]

353   improving our knowledge of deep mycorrhizal symbioses. A recent survey showed the [SOURCE: Page_16]

354   presence of AMF fungal spores in deep soil layers down to a depth of 8 m in pure and mixed- [SOURCE: Page_16]

355   species plantations of Eucalyptus grandis and Acacia mangium in Brazil (Pereira et al., 2018), [SOURCE: Page_16]

356   with root colonization rates between 6 and 25% for the 0-100 cm soil, and a decrease to about [SOURCE: Page_16]

357   10% below 1 meter and 5% - 6% below 3 m. This weak AMF colonization could be partly [SOURCE: Page_16]

16 [SOURCE: Page_16]

358   due to the age of the trees (over 1 year old) because the symbiosis with AM fungi is [SOURCE: Page_17]

359   commonly known to be substituted gradually by ectomycorrhizal fungi in trees plantations [SOURCE: Page_17]

360   (Read, 1991; Bellei et al., 1992; Boerner et al., 1996). [SOURCE: Page_17]

361   A vertical structuration of ectomycorrhizal (ECM) symbioses has been shown in numerous [SOURCE: Page_17]

362   studies, with different ECM fungal communities between organic and mineral soil layers [SOURCE: Page_17]

363   (Moyersoen et al., 1998; Dickie et al., 2002; Rosling et al., 2003; Genney et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_17]

364   Unfortunately, most of the studies do not exceed the depth of 2 m (Bakker et al., 2006; [SOURCE: Page_17]

365   Clemmensen et al., 2015; Rocha et al., 2016). ECM root tips have been observed down to a [SOURCE: Page_17]

366   depth of 4 m in bedrock fissures (Bornyasz et al., 2005; Querejeta et al., 2007). As for AM [SOURCE: Page_17]

367   fungi, the presence of ECM fungi propagules has been shown in very deep soil layers, down [SOURCE: Page_17]

368   to 10 m (Santana et al., 2016). Robin et al. (2019) demonstrated for the first time using [SOURCE: Page_17]

369   molecular sequencing that ECM symbioses occurred with Eucalyptus grandis roots at a depth [SOURCE: Page_17]

370   of 4 m in Brazil (Figure 6). The intraspecific diversity of the fungus Pisolithus in this study [SOURCE: Page_17]

371   was strongly impacted by the depth, suggesting the presence of a reservoir of biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_17]

372   associated with the deepest roots. [SOURCE: Page_17]

373   The occurrence of mycorrhizal associations (with both AM and ECM fungi) in deep soil [SOURCE: Page_17]

374   layers can contribute to increasing the volume of soil explored by scarce roots, which can [SOURCE: Page_17]

375   greatly enhance water and nutrient capture during drought periods (Lehto and Zwiazek, 2011; [SOURCE: Page_17]

376   Begum et al., 2019). Moreover, mycorrhizal fungi have a strong impact on carbon cycling [SOURCE: Page_17]

377   (Rillig et al., 2001; Tedersoo and Bahram, 2019), with possible consequences on C [SOURCE: Page_17]

378   sequestration in deep soil layers. For example, the symbiosis with ECM fungi increases the [SOURCE: Page_17]

379   lifespan of fine roots (King et al., 2002; Guo et al., 2008; McCormack and Guo, 2014; [SOURCE: Page_17]

380   Lambais et al., 2017), and the fungal biomass of both AM and ECM fungi can represent a [SOURCE: Page_17]

381   significant C sink in forest ecosystems (Rillig et al., 2001; Wilson et al., 2009; Cairney, 2012; [SOURCE: Page_17]

382   Clemmensen et al., 2015). The turnover of fungal biomass in deep soil layers can be lower [SOURCE: Page_17]

17 [SOURCE: Page_17]

383   than in the topsoil as a result of the low abundance of predatory organisms (Fernandez et al., [SOURCE: Page_18]

384   2016), which suggests that the contribution of fungi to C storage in the subsoil could be [SOURCE: Page_18]

385   important. [SOURCE: Page_18]

386   There are serious technical and methodological difficulties to study mycorrhizal associations [SOURCE: Page_18]

387   with deep roots, which explains that mycorrhizal symbioses in deep soil layers are still very [SOURCE: Page_18]

388   little documented. The difficulty to sample a sufficient weight of mycorrhizal fungi in [SOURCE: Page_18]

389   undisturbed soil at large depth is a major challenge. Some authors speculated that they did not [SOURCE: Page_18]

390   sample deeply enough to determine the limits of ECM fine roots (Bakker et al., 2006). The [SOURCE: Page_18]

391   minirhizotron method is well suited for root observations and has already been used to study [SOURCE: Page_18]

392   the dynamics of ECM fungi (Allen and Kitajima, 2013; McCormack et al., 2017). A recent [SOURCE: Page_18]

393   study using minirhizotrons showed typical ECM structures associated with deep roots down [SOURCE: Page_18]

394   to 6 m in a Brazilian eucalypt plantation (Lambais et al., 2017). However, the method itself [SOURCE: Page_18]

395   could affect mycorrhization, especially in deep soil layers (transport of spores, modification [SOURCE: Page_18]

396   of the environment near the tube…) and might create a favorable environment for fungi [SOURCE: Page_18]

397   growth. These interesting observations, despite their limitations, suggest the urgent need to [SOURCE: Page_18]

398   combine several methods making it possible to study in situ simultaneously the dynamics of [SOURCE: Page_18]

399   roots and mycorrhizal symbioses. We also need to go beyond diversity approaches and to [SOURCE: Page_18]

400   study more the functional aspects of these symbioses. Although a growing body of evidence [SOURCE: Page_18]

401   suggests that biological activity in deep soil layers is a major scientific lock poorly [SOURCE: Page_18]

402   investigated and important to resolve to improve our understanding of the biogeochemistry of [SOURCE: Page_18]

403   forest ecosystems, it remains a major methodological and technical challenge (Gocke et al., [SOURCE: Page_18]

404   2017). [SOURCE: Page_18]

405                5. Water and nutrient uptake in deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_18]

18 [SOURCE: Page_18]

406   The role of deep roots on water uptake by trees is well documented (Phillips et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_19]

407   Deep roots are essential to sustain high tree transpiration over dry seasons, which strongly [SOURCE: Page_19]

408   influences the Amazonian climate (Kleidon and Heimann, 2000; Saleska et al., 2007). Soil [SOURCE: Page_19]

409   water content monitoring throughout soil profiles as well as studies using hydrogen and [SOURCE: Page_19]

410   oxygen isotopes at specific dates make it possible to estimate at which depth water is taken up [SOURCE: Page_19]

411   by tree roots (Guderle and Hildebrandt, 2015; Beyer et al., 2016; Koeniger et al., 2016; [SOURCE: Page_19]

412   Trogisch et al., 2016; Beyer et al., 2018). Stahl et al. (2013) showed that 46% of the trees [SOURCE: Page_19]

413   were extracting water at a depth of at least 1.2 m in a tropical rainforest. Trees have developed [SOURCE: Page_19]

414   efficient strategies for the acquisition of water resources in deep soil layers, which can also [SOURCE: Page_19]

415   help sustain shallow root functioning during drought through hydraulic redistribution [SOURCE: Page_19]

416   (Burgess et al., 1998; Domec et al., 2004; Bleby et al., 2010). Burgess et al. (2001) have [SOURCE: Page_19]

417   demonstrated that Eucalyptus species have the capacity to redistribute water from wetter deep [SOURCE: Page_19]

418   layers into shallow soil layers through vertical and horizontal roots. Hydraulic redistribution [SOURCE: Page_19]

419   has now been demonstrated for many tree species and modeling approaches showed that this [SOURCE: Page_19]

420   process can strongly influence the evapotranspiration and the net carbon exchange of the [SOURCE: Page_19]

421   ecosystem (Domec et al., 2010). Water uptake in very deep soil layers is highly dynamic in [SOURCE: Page_19]

422   response to changes in atmospheric demand and soil water contents in the upper layers [SOURCE: Page_19]

423   (Christina et al., 2017). A modeling approach showed that the occurrence of rainfall events, [SOURCE: Page_19]

424   changing from a week to another, had a strong impact on which depth water uptake occurs [SOURCE: Page_19]

425   (Figure 7). It has also been shown that tree nutrition can greatly influence water withdrawal [SOURCE: Page_19]

426   by roots in very deep soil layers (Christina et al., 2018). Recently, Li et al. (2019) showed that [SOURCE: Page_19]

427   the extraction of water in deep soil layers by the root system of apple stands between 15 and [SOURCE: Page_19]

428   22 years of age was mainly occurring at depths below 12 m in a sub-humid region of China. [SOURCE: Page_19]

429   They also suggested that roots in the 1-12 m layer remained alive to transport the water taken [SOURCE: Page_19]

430   up below 12 m as they were probably unable to extract additional water in the upper 12 m. [SOURCE: Page_19]

19 [SOURCE: Page_19]

431   The access of roots to a very deep water table can dramatically change tree functioning and [SOURCE: Page_20]

432   the net C exchanges in forest ecosystems (Roupsard et al., 1999; Carter and White, 2009; [SOURCE: Page_20]

433   Zolfaghar et al., 2017). To improve soil-vegetation-atmosphere transfer models in tropical [SOURCE: Page_20]

434   forest ecosystems, we need a better understanding of the potential of very deep roots to [SOURCE: Page_20]

435   withdraw water stored in the subsoil (Akkermans et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_20]

436   The contribution of deep roots to supply tree requirements has been much less studied for [SOURCE: Page_20]

437   nutrients than for water. A safety net of tree roots reducing the losses of nutrients by deep [SOURCE: Page_20]

438   leaching is well known in agroforestry systems (Allen et al., 2004). Monitoring soil solution [SOURCE: Page_20]

439   chemistry throughout very deep soil profiles showed that Eucalyptus roots take up most of the [SOURCE: Page_20]

440   nutrients dissolved in the gravitational solutions before leaching at great depth (Laclau et al., [SOURCE: Page_20]

441   2010; Mareschal et al., 2013), with a very quick uptake by a root mat during the [SOURCE: Page_20]

442   decomposition processes in the forest floor (Laclau et al., 2004). Sr and Ca cycling were also [SOURCE: Page_20]

443   very conservative in a tropical rainforest in French Guyana, with very little Sr contribution [SOURCE: Page_20]

444   from deep soil layers for tree species differing by their rooting depth, which supported the [SOURCE: Page_20]

445   theory that Sr and Ca uptake and cycling are mostly superficial in tropical rainforests (Poszwa [SOURCE: Page_20]

446   et al., 2002). Recent studies using markers suggest that a functional specialization of fine [SOURCE: Page_20]

447   roots could also influence their ability to take up nutrients depending on the depth. Comparing [SOURCE: Page_20]

448   oak, European beech and Norway spruce monocultures in Denmark, Göransson et al. (2006) [SOURCE: Page_20]

449   showed that the distribution of roots and mycelia was not a reliable indicator of nutrient [SOURCE: Page_20]

450   uptake capacity by tree roots at different soil depths. The uptake capacity from deep soil [SOURCE: Page_20]

451   layers was higher than expected from the root distribution for most of the markers compared [SOURCE: Page_20]

452   with the topsoil, which might be explained by an overlap of the uptake zones around the roots [SOURCE: Page_20]

453   and mycorrhizal hyphae in the topsoil (Göransson et al., 2006). Injection of 15NO$_3$−, Rb+ (K+ [SOURCE: Page_20]

454   analog) and Sr$_2$+ (Ca$_2$+ analog) markers at the depths of 10, 50, 150 and 300 cm in Brazilian [SOURCE: Page_20]

455   Ferralsol soils also suggested a functional specialization of fine roots with a higher capacity to [SOURCE: Page_20]

20 [SOURCE: Page_20]

456   take up cations in deep soil layers, in 2- and 6-year-old Eucalyptus grandis plantations (Da [SOURCE: Page_21]

457   Silva et al., 2011; Bordron et al., 2019). Trees exhibit a great flexibility in their capability to [SOURCE: Page_21]

458   adjust their root development and the uptake of soil resource in the layers where they are [SOURCE: Page_21]

459   available (Jackson et al., 1990; Hutchings and de Kroon, 1994; Robinson, 1996). A recent [SOURCE: Page_21]

460   study in a rainfall exclusion experiment showed that eucalypt fine root functioning can be [SOURCE: Page_21]

461   considerably altered as a response to drought in deep soil profiles (Pradier et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_21]

462   Whereas H3O+ and exchangeable K+ concentrations were not influenced by the drought [SOURCE: Page_21]

463   treatment (37% of rainfall exclusion) in the bulk soil, they were significantly higher in the [SOURCE: Page_21]

464   rhizosphere than in the bulk soil in plots with reduced rainfall, notably at a depth of more than [SOURCE: Page_21]

465   2 m. This study suggested that root-induced weathering of K-bearing minerals contributed to [SOURCE: Page_21]

466   increasing rhizosphere acidification and K bioavailability at great depth. [SOURCE: Page_21]

467               6. Influence of soil environment and resource availability [SOURCE: Page_21]

468   Deep roots are highly plastic in response to heterogeneous resource distributions (Hodge et [SOURCE: Page_21]

469   al., 2009). Plants have the ability to adapt their root system to changing soil water contents, [SOURCE: Page_21]

470   nutrient availabilities, soil physical properties, and soil atmosphere composition. The [SOURCE: Page_21]

471   variability of environmental factors affecting root growth are buffered in deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_21]

472   compared to the topsoil (Voroney, 2007). Changes in fine root structure, architecture, and [SOURCE: Page_21]

473   production with soil depth can be influenced by the spatial heterogeneity of soil resources and [SOURCE: Page_21]

474   soil microclimate (Addo-Danso et al., 2020). Root ingrowth bags installed down to a depth of [SOURCE: Page_21]

475   6 m in mixed-species plantations of Acacia mangium and Eucalyptus grandis showed a [SOURCE: Page_21]

476   remarkable capacity of very deep roots to explore resource-rich soil patches despite their low [SOURCE: Page_21]

477   density in the soil (Germon et al., 2018). A high plasticity of fine roots has also been shown [SOURCE: Page_21]

478   through a vertical segregation of fine roots in mixed-species forests making it possible to [SOURCE: Page_21]

479   explore more intensively deep soil profiles than monospecific stands (Schenk et al., 1999). [SOURCE: Page_21]

480   Some studies in agroforestry systems and forest ecosystems suggest that fine roots of the [SOURCE: Page_21]

21 [SOURCE: Page_21]

481   weakest competitor can be partially excluded from soil horizons where the resources limiting [SOURCE: Page_22]

482   tree growth are concentrated (Schmid and Kazda, 2002; Mulia and Dupraz, 2006; Laclau et [SOURCE: Page_22]

483   al., 2013a). [SOURCE: Page_22]

484   Plant species use a large range of rooting patterns to cope with periodic drought, from [SOURCE: Page_22]

485   "drought tolerant strategies" with fine roots surviving in periodically dry soil to "drought [SOURCE: Page_22]

486   avoiding strategies" shedding fine roots from dry soil horizons while rapidly developing roots [SOURCE: Page_22]

487   in moister areas (Brunner et al., 2015; Vries et al., 2016; Bristiel et al., 2018). Soil water [SOURCE: Page_22]

488   content is a major driver of root growth and production in deep soil layers. Adriano et al. [SOURCE: Page_22]

489   (2017) showed that fine root mass of Citrus sinensis were higher in deep soil layers (between [SOURCE: Page_22]

490   the depths of 5 m and 8 m) for rainfed trees than for irrigated trees. However, changes in SRL [SOURCE: Page_22]

491   and SRA along soil profiles were unclear in those Citrus plantations, whatever the water [SOURCE: Page_22]

492   supply regime. Schenk and Jackson (2002) showed that the maximum rooting depth in [SOURCE: Page_22]

493   tropical ecosystems was positively correlated with the length of the dry season and negatively [SOURCE: Page_22]

494   correlated with the annual precipitation. Wang et al. (2015) also found a highly negative [SOURCE: Page_22]

495   correlation between root mass and soil water content down to a depth of 21 m in grasslands [SOURCE: Page_22]

496   and shrublands in China. Drought periods commonly increase the proportion of fine roots in [SOURCE: Page_22]

497   deep soil layers as well as specific root areas (Markesteijn and Poorter, 2009; Ma et al., 2018; [SOURCE: Page_22]

498   Zhou et al., 2018). In a survey of 62 tropical tree species, Markesteijn and Poorter (2009) [SOURCE: Page_22]

499   showed that trees increase the belowground biomass and the proportion of deep roots in [SOURCE: Page_22]

500   response to dry conditions. Germon et al. (2019) showed an increase in SRL and SRA down [SOURCE: Page_22]

501   to a depth of 16 m in response to throughfall exclusion for Eucalyptus trees in Brazil. [SOURCE: Page_22]

502   Fine root dynamics can also be influenced by the depth of the water table. Li et al. (2015) [SOURCE: Page_22]

503   showed that the displacement of the root front of Alhagi sparsifolia was 0.66 cm d-1 when the [SOURCE: Page_22]

504   water table was at a depth of 0.8 m and 1.5 cm d-1 when the water table was at a depth of 2.2 [SOURCE: Page_22]

505   m. Canham et al. (2012) showed a seasonal distribution of fine root biomass in deep soil [SOURCE: Page_22]

22 [SOURCE: Page_22]

506   layers in response to the fluctuations of the depth of the water table in southwest Australia. [SOURCE: Page_23]

507   Although the physiological mechanisms remain little understood, some studies investigating [SOURCE: Page_23]

508   the relationships between changes in water table depth and fine root dynamics suggest that an [SOURCE: Page_23]

509   increase in water table depth could promote an increase in root elongation rates downwards [SOURCE: Page_23]

510   (Stave et al., 2005; Canham, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_23]

511   The influence of soil texture on fine root distributions remains unclear. Fine root distributions [SOURCE: Page_23]

512   were shallower in sandy soils than in clayey soils in a tropical evergreen forest (Schenk and [SOURCE: Page_23]

513   Jackson (2002). However, roots of Haloxyolon ammodendron reached a depth of 10 m in a [SOURCE: Page_23]

514   sandy soil and only 3 m in a heavy soil (Xu and Li, 2009). Comparing root distributions for [SOURCE: Page_23]

515   Entandrophragma cylindricum Sprague (Meliaceae) in African semi-deciduous rainforests, [SOURCE: Page_23]

516   Freycon et al. (2015) showed that fine root densities down to a depth of 6 m were lower in a [SOURCE: Page_23]

517   coarse-textured Arenosol soil than in a fine-textured Ferralsol soil. The same pattern was [SOURCE: Page_23]

518   showed by Laclau et al. (2013b) comparing fine root densities down to a depth of 10 m in [SOURCE: Page_23]

519   sandy and clayey Ferralsol soils in the same Eucalyptus stand. Similarly, Robinson et al. [SOURCE: Page_23]

520   (2006) in Australia reported that eucalypt species can reach 8-10 m deep and withdraw deep [SOURCE: Page_23]

521   soil water (Figure 8) despite a high bulk density (up to 2.0 g cm-3) in clayey subsoil. Canadell [SOURCE: Page_23]

522   et al. (1996) cited some examples of roots reaching great depths in rocky soils, using cracks [SOURCE: Page_23]

523   and macropores at the vicinity of rocks to grow with low mechanical constraints. [SOURCE: Page_23]

524   Nutrient availability can also influence fine root traits throughout deep soil profiles. Studying [SOURCE: Page_23]

525   fine roots and nutrient distributions down to a depth of 10 m in five semi-arid and arid forests [SOURCE: Page_23]

526   in the southwestern USA, McCulley et al. (2004) showed morphological and physiological [SOURCE: Page_23]

527   adjustments of fine roots in response to nutrient availability. The fate of tracers injected at [SOURCE: Page_23]

528   various depth in a Ferralsol soil suggested that NPK fertilization increased the capacity of [SOURCE: Page_23]

529   Eucalyptus grandis fine roots to take up nutrients down to a depth of 3 m (Bordron et al., [SOURCE: Page_23]

530   2019). Moreover, NPK fertilization increased fine root density, specific root length and [SOURCE: Page_23]

23 [SOURCE: Page_23]

531   specific root area throughout the soil profile. However, N fertilization did not influence the [SOURCE: Page_24]

532   fine root biomass down to a depth of 3 m in another E. grandis stand at the same site in Brazil [SOURCE: Page_24]

533   (Jourdan et al., 2008). The relationships between nutrient availability throughout deep soil [SOURCE: Page_24]

534   profiles, fine root dynamics and fine root traits are difficult to disentangle from changes in [SOURCE: Page_24]

535   environmental conditions and further studies are needed to gain insight into the specific effect [SOURCE: Page_24]

536   of soil nutrient contents. [SOURCE: Page_24]

537   Even though the consequences of elevated CO$_2$ concentrations in the atmosphere on plant [SOURCE: Page_24]

538   growth has been studied in many FACE experiments, fine root responses are still little [SOURCE: Page_24]

539   documented for trees. In a review paper, Iversen (2010) showed that high atmospheric CO$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_24]

540   concentrations tend to increase root depth in forest ecosystems. However, most of the studies [SOURCE: Page_24]

541   dealing with fine roots in FACE experiments were carried out in the upper soil layers. Fine [SOURCE: Page_24]

542   root traits influence tree functioning (Prieto et al., 2015; Addo-Danso et al., 2020) but the [SOURCE: Page_24]

543   scarcity of information on the influence of the rise of atmospheric CO$_2$ concentrations on fine [SOURCE: Page_24]

544   roots traits at depths of more than 30 cm limits our understanding of the response of forests to [SOURCE: Page_24]

545   climate change on huge areas. Studies dealing with the influence of elevated CO$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_24]

546   concentrations on root distributions and fine root traits in deep soil layers are therefore [SOURCE: Page_24]

547   urgently needed for tree species. [SOURCE: Page_24]

548               7. Modeling approaches to gain insight into the functioning of deep fine [SOURCE: Page_24]

549                   roots [SOURCE: Page_24]

550   Modeling architecture development and dynamics of the deep fine roots is hindered by the [SOURCE: Page_24]

551   limited data available in the literature, yet necessary to parametrize and evaluate the models. [SOURCE: Page_24]

552   Root number, root diameter, root volume, and root radial distances are the main parameters of [SOURCE: Page_24]

553   root system biomechanics (Nicoll and Ray, 1996; Danquechin Dorval et al., 2016), varying [SOURCE: Page_24]

554   considerably with soil layers. Soil depth strongly influences the root system architecture due [SOURCE: Page_24]

24 [SOURCE: Page_24]

555   to the heterogeneity of its environment. Root architectural models could simulate accurately [SOURCE: Page_25]

556   spatial and temporal dynamics through a simulation of the root system in 3D. Integrating a [SOURCE: Page_25]

557   vertically discretized soil into a model is particularly important as shallow and deep roots [SOURCE: Page_25]

558   present different spatial and temporal dynamics. Model parametrization needs to consider [SOURCE: Page_25]

559   shallow and deep-rooted systems behaving differently according to the environmental factors [SOURCE: Page_25]

560   and soil conditions. These models can be parametrized according to the definition of root [SOURCE: Page_25]

561   types (e.g. RootTyp from Pagès et al. (2004); or DigR (Figure 9) from Barczi et al. (2018)) or [SOURCE: Page_25]

562   by branching order (e.g. OpenSimRoot from Postma et al. (2017)) or through parametric L- [SOURCE: Page_25]

563   system modeling (Leitner et al., 2010), which also might vary with soil layers. Such a model [SOURCE: Page_25]

564   without discretized processes by soil layers is not capable to simulate accurately the impact of [SOURCE: Page_25]

565   environmental conditions and soil resources on root development. Models devoted to [SOURCE: Page_25]

566   simulating water and nutrient uptake dynamics need to integrate the functional specialization [SOURCE: Page_25]

567   of deep roots and root growth plasticity in response to environmental conditions, in particular [SOURCE: Page_25]

568   in deep soil layers. However, most root functioning rules are still not completely understood, [SOURCE: Page_25]

569   especially at great depth as a result of the difficulty of measuring those processes in situ. [SOURCE: Page_25]

570   Therefore, model calibration must cope with this knowledge and experimental limits. [SOURCE: Page_25]

571   Architectural models can also be coupled with models simulating nutrient uptake (Mayer, [SOURCE: Page_25]

572   1999; Bonneu et al., 2012; Mayer et al., 2012), water transport (Doussan et al., 1998), [SOURCE: Page_25]

573   rhizospheric processes (Kim and Silk, 1999), or even reactive transport models (Mayer, 1999; [SOURCE: Page_25]

574   Mayer et al., 2012; Gérard et al., 2017). Nevertheless, it can be convenient to simulate [SOURCE: Page_25]

575   accurately the overall architecture and any specialized functions of deep roots compared to [SOURCE: Page_25]

576   shallow roots. [SOURCE: Page_25]

577   Furthermore, deep roots have a strong influence on deep carbon stocks, which accounts for a [SOURCE: Page_25]

578   significant amount of carbon (Jobbágy and Jackson, 2004) and can be partially renewed at the [SOURCE: Page_25]

579   10-year scale (Baisden and Parfitt, 2007; Koarashi et al., 2012). Models predicting soil [SOURCE: Page_25]

25 [SOURCE: Page_25]

580   organic carbon stocks through a single soil layer modeling approach (Hansen et al., 1991; [SOURCE: Page_26]

581   Sallih and Pansu, 1993; Petersen et al., 2005; Pansu et al., 2010; Oelbermann and Voroney, [SOURCE: Page_26]

582   2011) are therefore not considering adequately deep roots that play an active role in carbon [SOURCE: Page_26]

583   cycling. In recent years, an awareness of the importance of deep carbon stocks by the [SOURCE: Page_26]

584   modeling community has led to the development of different discretized models depending on [SOURCE: Page_26]

585   the soil depth (Braakhekke et al., 2011; Braakhekke et al., 2013; Guenet et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_26]

586   Taghizadeh-Toosi et al., 2014). However, modeling accurately deep root dynamics and their [SOURCE: Page_26]

587   active role in C fluxes in forest ecosystems, as well as nutrient and water uptake throughout [SOURCE: Page_26]

588   tree growth remains a major challenge for the next decades. [SOURCE: Page_26]

589   Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_26]

590   Deep roots are a major component of forest ecosystems that can greatly influence the [SOURCE: Page_26]

591   biogeochemistry of the Critical Zone. Their anatomical, morphological and functional root [SOURCE: Page_26]

592   traits may be different from those of shallow roots (Figure 2; Appendix 1), with a major [SOURCE: Page_26]

593   orientation towards the acquisition and transport of water. Climatic factors affect root [SOURCE: Page_26]

594   phenology throughout deep soil profiles differently, as the soil environment is buffered in the [SOURCE: Page_26]

595   deep soil layers. More research is needed to gain insight into the relationships between fine [SOURCE: Page_26]

596   root dynamics and their anatomical, architectural and functional characteristics along deep [SOURCE: Page_26]

597   soil profiles for a wide range of species, climates and soil properties. A high plasticity of fine [SOURCE: Page_26]

598   roots has been shown in response to changing soil conditions from topsoil to deep soil layers [SOURCE: Page_26]

599   (Appendix 1) but the control of fine root dynamics in very deep soil layers remains little [SOURCE: Page_26]

600   understood despite their crucial role during dry periods on huge areas of forest worldwide. [SOURCE: Page_26]

601   Research efforts should be directed to understanding the deep-root dynamics and associated [SOURCE: Page_26]

602   functions that could have an important influence on climate change mitigation. [SOURCE: Page_26]

1301   Figure 7. Daily simulated actual evapotranspiration (AETsim) using MAESPA model (A) and 1302   time series of root length density (RLD, cm dm-3) (B) over 5 years after planting Eucalyptus 1303   trees in Brazil. The AETsim (A) is represented as a function of the depth of root water uptake 1304   (RWUsim, red gradient) or uptake from the capillary fringe above the water table (WT, blue), 1305   and the evaporation from the soil surface and from the wet canopy is represented in brown. 1306   Vertical lines (B) indicate the days when root profiles were measured, and the green line is the 1307   maximum measured rooting depth. Adapted from Christina et al. (2017), used by permission. 1308 [SOURCE: Page_48]
